All rights reserved.Paid ContentLifeChronic Hepatitis B Affects Millions Worldwide – So Why Do People Know So Little About It?A new survey from biopharmaceutical company GSK highlights awareness as being one of the biggest barriers to tackling the public health threat that chronic hepatitis B presents around the globe.1GSK28/07/2026 03:27pm BSTThis article was created and funded by GSKWhat's this?This content was paid for by an advertiser. It was produced by our commercial team and did not involve HuffPost editorial staff.Richard Moran Photography LtdChronic hepatitis B is not something most people think about in their day-to-day lives.1 But, globally, it remains a major health issue – and one that health leaders say is not improving fast enough.2Chronic hepatitis B is a long-term liver infection caused by the hepatitis B virus, which can increase the risk of developing severe liver damage (cirrhosis) and liver cancer.3 People can become infected with the hepatitis B virus through exposure to infected blood and other bodily fluids.2 Most people do not experience any symptoms when initially infected with the hepatitis B virus, and the infection can go unnoticed for many years.2˒3It is estimated that approximately 240 million people are living with chronic hepatitis B globally.2˒3 Yet despite the burden, the World Health Organization (WHO) states that efforts to eliminate hepatitis B as a public health threat by 2030 are currently off track.2While tools like vaccinations, testing and treatment already exist, progress has not kept pace with what is possible.2Challenging a lack of awareness of chronic hepatitis BAgainst that backdrop, a new global online survey commissioned and funded by GSK and conducted among 5,000 general public adults across five countries suggests that one of the biggest challenges may be something much simpler: awareness.1 Despite affecting hundreds of millions of people worldwide,2 chronic hepatitis B remains out of sight for many.1 More than eight in 10 adults surveyed say they were either unaware of chronic hepatitis or knew very little about it.1 The lack of awareness goes beyond simply not recognising the disease name. Many people are unclear on basic facts – from how the virus is prevented (such as through vaccination, and reducing exposure to blood and other bodily fluids that may carry the hepatitis B virus3) and if treatment is available, to how it impacts long-term health.1 Chronic hepatitis B: a major cancer risk flying under the radarOne of the most striking findings from the survey is the gap between what is known medically about chronic hepatitis B and how it is perceived by the public.1 Chronic hepatitis B is a leading cause of liver cancer, estimated to be the cause of around half of liver cancer cases globally.2˒4 Yet only approximately one in three people in the GSK survey considered it to be a high-risk factor.1 By comparison, far more people associated lifestyle and environmental risk factors like smoking, alcohol use, obesity and air pollution with cancer.1 Even among those who are aware of a link between chronic hepatitis B and liver cancer, the scale of the risk is often underestimated. On average, the adults surveyed thought chronic hepatitis B accounted for around a quarter of liver cancer cases, significantly lower than current global estimates.Richard Moran Photography LtdWhy low awareness about chronic hepatitis B can delay actionLow awareness does not just affect what people know. It also shapes how they think and what they do.Around three in four adults surveyed said they had never been tested for hepatitis B.1 Among adults who have never been tested, the leading barriers are simply never having considered it and not thinking they were at risk – not active refusal.1 When something isn’t widely discussed and commonly understood, it can be harder to place in a personal context.Increasing the visibility of chronic hepatitis BWhat makes this issue more striking is that chronic hepatitis B is a disease we know a great deal about and can manage.Vaccination, testing and treatment for hepatitis B are well established.1 Yet globally, outcomes are not improving at the pace expected, with low treatment coverage and delayed diagnosis still contributing to rising numbers of deaths.1The WHO’s latest assessment suggests that the gap today is less about whether solutions exist, and more about how consistently they are used in practice, and how much priority is given to addressing the condition by healthcare decision makers, including policymakers. Closing that gap may depend, in part, on making the condition feel more visible and more relevant to people’s lives.1Making hepatitis B part of the conversationChronic hepatitis B is too often overlooked, with consequences that can be serious for individuals, families and communities.1˒2 Greater awareness can help change that.Breaking the chain starts by breaking the silence. By talking more openly, understanding whether testing may be relevant, and knowing how to help protect yourself and others, we can take an important first step.Because when something becomes part of the conversation, it becomes easier to recognise when it matters. Talk. Test. Protect.To learn more about living with chronic hepatitis B, read Natalia’s story on GSK.com.References: GSK Global Chronic Hepatitis B Awareness 2026 Survey, data on file.World Health Organization. 3 million Ghanaians living with hepatitis B - GHS

Hepatitis B-related deaths rose from 15,443 in 2024 to approximately 16,000 in 2025. Mother-to-child transmission accounts for 95% of infections, though infant vaccination coverage has reached 95%. The government is advancing plans for a birth-dose vaccination program and national hepatitis control policy to meet WHO elimination targets by 2030. Northern regions bear the highest burden, and public awareness campaigns urge testing and vaccination.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

3 million Ghanaians living with hepatitis B - GHS

News Brief
Ghana Health Service reports that 3 million Ghanaians are living with chronic hepatitis B, with only 1% diagnosed and less than 1% receiving treatment, leading to preventable deaths and severe liver complications. Hepatitis B-related deaths rose from 15,443 in 2024 to approximately 16,000 in 2025. Mother-to-child transmission accounts for 95% of infections, though infant vaccination coverage has reached 95%. The government is advancing plans for a birth-dose vaccination program and national hepatitis control policy to meet WHO elimination targets by 2030. Northern regions bear the highest burden, and public awareness campaigns urge testing and vaccination.

Please wait, loading captcha By continuing, you agree to the Terms & Privacy Policy Self Service Advert Sitemap Partners About Us Sign up Login Home News Sports Business Entertainment Africa GhanaWeb TV Opinions Memories Cartoons Say It Loud Real Estate Home - HealthArchived Health NewsHospitalsStatisticsTourists InfoHIV/AIDSIntroduction to NHISNHIS - Official SiteGeneral News More Crime & PunishmentPoliticsRegionalHealthGhanaians AbroadAfrica NewsReligion News You are here: Home → News → Health → 2026 07 29 → Article 2045444 Health News of Wednesday, 29 July 2026 DisclaimerSource: ghanaiantimes.com.gh 2026-07-293 million Ghanaians living with hepatitis B - GHS « PrevNext »Comments (0) Listen to Article Share: Dr Samuel Kaba Akoriyea is the Director-General of Ghana Health Service Discover moreMedical Facilities & ServicesvaccineMedical Literature & ResourcesVaccines & ImmunizationsvaccinesHistoryPublic HealthInfectious Diseases Only one per cent of the estimated three million people living with chronic hepatitis B in Ghana have been diagnosed. The figure falls far short of the World Health Organisation (WHO) target of diagnosing 90 per cent of people living with chronic viral hepatitis and placing 80 per cent of patients on treatment by 2030. Currently, less than one per cent of people diagnosed with hepatitis B in Ghana are receiving treatment, a situation health authorities say is fuelling preventable deaths and exposing many infected persons to severe liver complications. The Programme Manager of the National Viral Hepatitis Control Programme of the Ghana Health Service (GHS), Dr John Gerald Adiiboka, disclosed this at the commemoration of World Hepatitis Day in Accra yesterday. On the theme, “Hepatitis: Let’s break it down,” this year’s observance calls on countries to remove the barriers preventing people from accessing life-saving hepatitis prevention, testing and treatment services. Presenting the burden of viral hepatitis in Ghana, Dr Adiiboka said hepatitis B-related deaths increased from 15,443 in 2024 to about 16,000 in 2025. He said about 500,000 people were also living with hepatitis C, with deaths rising from about 2,040 in 2024 to approximately 2,086 in 2025. “Most people living with viral hepatitis in Ghana do not know they have the disease because it is largely asymptomatic. Many only report to the hospital when the liver has already been severely damaged,” he said. Dr Adiiboka said the northern parts of the country continued to bear the greatest burden of viral hepatitis, with the Savannah, Northern, Upper West and Upper East regions recording some of the highest prevalence rates. He explained that mother-to-child transmission accounted for about 95 per cent of hepatitis B infections in Ghana, making interventions targeting newborns critical to breaking the chain of transmission. Nonetheless, the programme manager said Ghana was making significant progress in preventing new infections, noting that coverage of the third dose of the hepatitis B vaccine among infants had reached about 95 per cent, exceeding the WHO target of 90 per cent. He said plans were far advanced to roll out a nationwide hepatitis B birth-dose vaccination programme to protect newborns immediately after birth, describing it as one of the most cost-effective interventions to prevent future infections and liver cancer. According to him, increased investment in testing, treatment and surveillance, the implementation of a national hepatitis control policy, and sustained political commitment would enable Ghana to achieve the WHO hepatitis elimination targets by 2030. The Minister of Health, Kwabena Mintah Akandoh, in a speech read on his behalf, reaffirmed the government’s commitment to strengthening efforts to eliminate viral hepatitis as a public health threat. He said the government would continue to strengthen vaccination to reduce mother-to-child transmission, expand equitable access to screening and diagnosis, improve laboratory and surveillance systems, decentralise hepatitis services and strengthen referral pathways. Charging members of the newly constituted Technical Working Group to support the implementation of a coordinated national viral hepatitis elimination plan and work hard to reduce the prevalence rate in the country, Mr Akandoh urged the public to know their hepatitis status by getting tested and vaccinated where eligible. “Our goal is a Ghana where no child acquires hepatitis at birth, no adult develops avoidable liver disease because of late diagnosis, and no family loses a loved one because essential prevention or treatment was beyond reach,” he said. World Hepatitis Day is observed annually on July 28 to raise awareness of viral hepatitis, an inflammation of the liver that can lead to chronic liver disease, cirrhosis and liver cancer. There are five main types of viral hepatitis—A, B, C, D and E—with hepatitis B and C accounting for the majority of chronic infections. Common symptoms include fatigue, fever, loss of appetite, abdominal pain, dark urine, pale stools, joint pain and jaundice, although many infected persons may not develop symptoms until the disease has advanced. Viral hepatitis is commonly transmitted through contact with infected blood or other bodily fluids, but safe and effective vaccines are available to prevent hepatitis A and B. 医生:工作文化、乙型肝炎发病率存在肝癌风险 - taipeitimes.com

Work culture, hepatitis B rates present liver cancer risk: doctors - taipeitimes.com
新闻简报台湾医生警告说,高要求的工作文化和高乙型肝炎发病率相结合会显着增加患肝癌的风险,特别是对于 40 岁以上的人。台湾癌症基金会的一项调查显示,台湾职场人士的生活方式令人担忧,包括长时间工作、睡眠质量差、不健康饮食和缺乏锻炼。许多肝炎患者忽视定期体检,有些人依赖无效的补充剂而不是循证的 治疗。该报告强调,工作压力、轮班不规律和社交饮酒是主要影响因素,金融和公共部门工作人员尤其容易受到影响。这凸显了台湾迫切需要更好的工作场所健康实践和肝炎管理。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-07-28。根据纪念世界肝炎日的新闻发布会,活动日期是昨天。
地点与对象
台湾,特别是台湾劳动人口,特别是40岁或以上以及1984年或之前出生的人。
事件
台湾癌症基金会的医生在台北举行的新闻发布会上报告说,台湾的工作文化和高乙型肝炎发病率给 40 岁或以上的人群带来了患肝癌的特殊风险。官员在新闻发布会上报道称,该事件已得到证实。
规模、影响与风险

A survey showed that a majority of high-risk working Taiwanese do not seek treatment for hepatitis B, undergo regular check-ups, or live a healthy lifestyle. Specific risks include 11% working over 50 hours a week, 13% sleeping less than six hours a day, 40% having at least one unhealthy dietary habit, over 60% not exercising, and over 10% taking ineffective supplements. 35.7% of hepatitis patients had not undergone regular screenings, and 25.3% reported being too busy at work to make doctor visits.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Work culture, hepatitis B rates present liver cancer risk: doctors - taipeitimes.com

News Brief
Taiwanese doctors warn that the combination of demanding work culture and high hepatitis B rates significantly increases liver cancer risk, especially for those over 40. A survey by the Formosa Cancer Foundation reveals alarming lifestyle patterns among working Taiwanese, including long hours, poor sleep, unhealthy diets, and lack of exercise. Many hepatitis patients neglect regular medical check-ups, with some relying on ineffective supplements instead of evidence-based treatment. The report highlights work-related stress, irregular shifts, and social drinking as key contributing factors, with finance and public sector workers particularly vulnerable. This underscores the urgent need for better workplace health practices and hepatitis management in Taiwan.
Original source text (English)
Work culture, hepatitis B rates present liver cancer risk: doctors- By Lin Chih-yi and Jonathan Chin / Staff reporter, with staff writer Taiwan’s work culture and high hepatitis B rates present a special risk of liver cancer to those aged 40 years or over, doctors said yesterday. They made the remarks at a news conference in Taipei marking World Hepatitis Day hosted by the Formosa Cancer Foundation. Local inoculation policies mean that those born in 1984 or before have relatively high rates of hepatitis B, which precedes 70 percent of liver cancer cases, National Taiwan University vice superintendent Kao Jia-horng (高嘉宏) said. Officials attend a news conference hosted by the Formosa Cancer Foundation in Taipei yesterday.Photo: Lin Chih-yi, Taipei Times A survey conducted by the foundation showed that a majority of working Taiwanese at high risk of liver cancer do not seek treatment for hepatitis B, undergo regular check-ups or live a healthy lifestyle, he said. Citing the poll, foundation deputy chief executive director Chang Chia-lun (張家崙) said 11 percent of Taiwanese reported working more than 50 hours a week, and 13 percent reported sleeping less than six hours a day. Jobs that put employees on work rotations through odd hours instead of regular work days are commonplace, he said. About 40 percent of respondents had at least one unhealthy dietary habit linked to liver cancer, including consuming sugary drinks, highly processed food, deep-fried or roasted foods, and not enough vegetables, Chang said. More than 60 percent of working Taiwanese do not exercise and more than 10 percent take useless nutritional supplements, herbal medicines or folk cures to strengthen their liver health, he said. Nearly 40 percent of Taiwanese with hepatitis B who use supplements reported that they stopped receiving treatment or medical check-ups because they believe the supplements can replace evidence-based medicine, he said. Hepatitis patients require regular blood screening, ultrasounds and check-ups to prevent disorders originating in the liver, yet 35.7 percent had not done so and 43.6 percent stopped because they felt fine, Chang said. Another 25.3 percent reported being too busy at work to make regular doctor’s visits, he said. Stress and anxiety from work, regularly eating food prepared at restaurants, irregular rotations and sleep deprivation, exhaustion from overwork, and excessive alcohol consumption due to work-related social events are drivers of liver cancer in Taiwan, he said. People employed in finance, insurance or the public sector are especially at risk from drinking too much at work-related gatherings, he added. - You might also like- First tax refunds to be deposited on Friday2026-07-28The first batch of individual income tax refunds for this tax season is to be deposited on Friday, covering more than 3.65 million cases and totaling NT$61.24 billion (US$1.89 billion), with an average refund of NT$16,745 per case, the National Taxation Bureau of Kaohsiung said. After the annual individual income tax filing deadline, regional tax bureaus process refunds in three batches based on the filing method used by taxpayers, the bureau said, adding that the three batches are scheduled for Friday, Oct. 30, and Jan. 20 next year. The storm would be closest to Taiwan tomorrow, and bring heavy rain to Hualien and Taitung counties, as well as the Hengchun Peninsula (恆春半島), it said. If the storm strengthens into a typhoon, it would be the 12th this year. As the storm has maintained a steady speed, it is expected to cross the Bashi Channel relatively quickly, CWA forecaster Tseng Chao-cheng (曾昭誠) said. Mission aims to support 1.5 lakh Hepatitis B screenings nationwide through a physician-led awareness and screening initiative
新闻简报印度正在启动一项全国性的医生主导的倡议,名为'击败肝炎任务',以应对乙型肝炎的显著诊断问题,该病估计影响该国4亿人。仅有3%的患者得到诊断,不到1%的患者接受治疗。该活动计划通过5,000多名医生筛查1.5万人,并通过8,500个筛查点扩大至2.5万人的筛查。这项倡议由Cipla和INASL领导,包括宣传活动和展示世界上最大肝脏模型的公共设施。早期诊断被强调为减少肝病并发症的关键,医疗专业人员正合作改善筛查可及性和患者结局。

事件
A physician-led awareness and screening initiative called 'Mission Defeat Hepatitis' was launched, aiming to support 1.5 lakh Hepatitis B screenings across India. The initiative involves over 5,000 doctors and is a collaboration between Cipla and the Indian National Association for the Study of the Liver (INASL).
规模、影响与风险
India accounts for nearly 12% of the global Hepatitis B burden, with an estimated 4 crore people living with chronic Hepatitis B infection. However, only around 3% are diagnosed, and less than 1% receive appropriate treatment. The initiative aims to screen 1.5 lakh individuals in the first phase, with plans to expand to over 2.5 lakh screenings through 8,500 screening camps nationwide.

该倡议目前正在进行中,医疗保健专业人员积极参与筛查和宣传活动。该活动因其规模而被载入国际记录大全。第一阶段正在进行中,计划做出更大的努力,以促进全国范围内更多的放映。来源是完整的原始报告正文。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Mission aims to support 1.5 lakh Hepatitis B screenings nationwide through a physician-led awareness and screening initiative

News Brief
India is launching a nationwide physician-led initiative, 'Mission Defeat Hepatitis,' to address the significant under-diagnosis of Hepatitis B, which affects an estimated 4 crore people in the country. With only 3% diagnosed and less than 1% treated, the campaign aims to screen 1.5 lakh individuals through over 5,000 doctors, expanding to 2.5 lakh screenings via 8,500 camps. Led by Cipla and INASL, the initiative includes awareness drives and a public installation featuring the world's largest liver model. Early diagnosis is emphasized as critical to reducing liver disease complications, with healthcare professionals collaborating to improve screening access and patient outcomes.
Original source text (English)
Mission aims to support 1.5 lakh Hepatitis B screenings nationwide through a physician-led awareness and screening initiative The campaign also unveiled the World's Largest Liver Model, created using Hepatitis B antigen test kit covers that will be used to screen 1.5 lakh individuals, serving as a public installation to reinforce the importance of timely screening and diagnosis. Written By: Sponsored | Published : July 30, 2026 12:08 PM IST Share Mumbai, July XX 2026 : Despite advances in diagnosis and treatment, Hepatitis B continues to remain one of India's under-recognised public health challenges. India accounts for nearly 12% of the global Hepatitis B burden , with an estimated 4 crore people living with chronic Hepatitis B infection . Yet only around 3% are diagnosed , while less than 1% receive appropriate treatment , leavingmillions unaware of their infection and at risk of developing serious liver disease. Recognising the importance of early diagnosis, Cipla has joined hands with Indian National Association for the Study of the Liver (INASL) to continue Mission Defeat Hepatitis . A nationwide initiative aims to raise awareness, encourage early diagnosis, and at expand access to Hepatitis B screening across India . As part of this year's World Hepatitis Day observance, more than 5,000 doctors across the country have joined the initiative to support 1.5 lakh Hepatitis B screenings. Recognising the scale of this physician-led awareness and screening drive, the campaign has also earned an entry into the International Book of Records. This marks the first phase of a larger effort to facilitate more than 2.5 lakh screenings through over 8,500 screening camps nationwide. The event brought together healthcare professionals, liver disease experts and Cipla leadership to reinforce the need for timely Hepatitis B screening and diagnosis. The campaign also unveiled the World's Largest Liver Model, created using Hepatitis B antigen test kit covers that will be used to screen 1.5 lakh individuals, serving as a public installation to reinforce the importance of timely screening and diagnosis. Commenting on the initiative, Dr. Jaideep Gogtay, Chief Medical Officer, Cipla Limited, said, "Hepatitis B remains one of the under-diagnosed public health challenges in India. While effective therapies are available, millions continue to live with the infection without knowing their status. We believe awareness and early diagnosis are the first steps towards changing this reality. Through Mission Defeat Hepatitis, we are bringing together the medical fraternity, scientific bodies and communities to encourage timely screening and help people access appropriate care. Our vision is simple, to ensure that every individual at risk has the opportunity to know their Hepatitis B status. Because only when we diagnose earlier can we truly change the course of the disease." Speaking on the initiative, Prof. Dr. Ajay Duseja, HOD PGI & INASL Secretary General, said, "The Indian National Association for the Study of the Liver (INASL) is a non-profit organisation committed to advancing the discipline of hepatology in India. Early diagnosis remains one of the most effective interventions in Hepatitis B management. Mission Defeat Hepatitis provides an opportunity for healthcare professionals across the country to work towards a shared goal of increasing awareness, encouraging screening and connecting patients to appropriate care. Together, we can make meaningful progress in reducing the burden of Hepatitis B in India." Dr. Amit Mandot (Director Dept of Hepatology & LICU: Gleneagles Hospital, Mumbai): " Early diagnosis is a cornerstone of effective Hepatitis B management, enabling timely intervention and significantly reducing the risk of advanced liver disease. Mission Defeat Hepatitis serves as a platform to unite healthcare professionals in a collective effort to enhance awareness, encourage screening, and facilitate early linkage to care. We at INASL & Gleneagles Hospital have active partnership in this initiative, particularly its support for screening programs and awareness-building activities that help drive early detection within the community. Through such collaborative efforts, we can accelerate progress toward reducing the burden of Hepatitis B in India and improving patient outcomes." Dr. Bipin Chevale, CEO of Gleneagles Hospital, " Hepatitis B continues to be one of the underdiagnosed liver diseases in India, despite being preventable and treatable with timely intervention. Our association with Mission Defeat Hepatitis Campaign highlights our commitment to promoting early screening and increasing public awareness, as early diagnosis remains the key to preventing serious liver complications. Through our experienced multidisciplinary team and state-of-the-art healthcare infrastructure, we strive to ensure that every patient has access to quality care, while contributing to the larger goal of reducing the burden of Hepatitis B in our communities, " Disclaimer: This article is from the Brand Desk. 世界卫生组织呼吁加大力度抗击乙型和丙型肝炎 - bluewin.ch

WHO Calls for Greater Efforts in the Fight Against Hepatitis B and C - bluewin.ch
新闻简报The WHO is urging increased global efforts to combat hepatitis B and C, which affect nearly 300 million people worldwide. Hepatitis B poses a greater burden, with 240 million cases and 1.1 million annual deaths, compared to 240,000 deaths from hepatitis C. While hepatitis C is largely curable, only 20% of patients have received treatment since 2015. In Switzerland, chronic hepatitis B affects 0.5-0.7% of the population, with acute cases declining. The WHO recommends routine testing for pregnant women and vaccination for newborns of infected mothers, while the FOPH advocates early vaccination and for at-risk groups. A recent study offers hope for a functional cure for hepatitis B through combination therapy.
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-07-28。证据中没有提到具体的事件日期。
地点与对象
Global: 287 million people with chronic hepatitis B or C. Switzerland: 0.5-0.7% of population chronically infected with hepatitis B; 0.1-0.4% with hepatitis C.
事件
WHO calls for greater efforts in fighting hepatitis B and C, including more research, testing, treatment, and political commitment. Hepatitis B poses a greater burden with 240 million cases and 1.1 million annual deaths.
规模、影响与风险
Global: 287 million chronic cases (hepatitis B or C). Hepatitis B: 240 million cases, 1.1 million annual deaths. Hepatitis C: 240,000 annual deaths. Switzerland: Declining acute hepatitis B cases and hepatitis C infections.
应对、状态与不确定性
世卫组织建议对孕妇进行常规检测,并对受感染母亲所生的婴儿进行 疫苗接种 检测。FOPH 建议早期 疫苗接种 和高危人群。不存在丙型肝炎 疫苗。最近的一项研究表明 功能性治愈 联合治疗乙型肝炎的潜力。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

WHO Calls for Greater Efforts in the Fight Against Hepatitis B and C - bluewin.ch

News Brief
The WHO is urging increased global efforts to combat hepatitis B and C, which affect nearly 300 million people worldwide. Hepatitis B poses a greater burden, with 240 million cases and 1.1 million annual deaths, compared to 240,000 deaths from hepatitis C. While hepatitis C is largely curable, only 20% of patients have received treatment since 2015. In Switzerland, chronic hepatitis B affects 0.5-0.7% of the population, with acute cases declining. The WHO recommends routine testing for pregnant women and vaccination for newborns of infected mothers, while the FOPH advocates early vaccination and for at-risk groups. A recent study offers hope for a functional cure for hepatitis B through combination therapy.
Original source text (English)
StartSportSwitzerlandInternationalFootballPeopleMyTechLifestyleNews HealthWHO Calls for Greater Efforts in the Fight Against Hepatitis B and CNearly 300 million people are chronically infected with the hepatitis B or C viruses. There is a vaccine against hepatitis B. (File photo)KeystoneIn the fight against hepatitis, the World Health Organization (WHO) is calling for more research, more testing and treatment, and stronger political commitment. According to the WHO, 287 million people worldwide are living with chronic hepatitis B or chronic hepatitis C.Keystone-SDA28.07.2026, 04:43These two diseases—which are not related—are caused by viruses that are transmitted primarily through blood. The liver inflammation resulting from the infection can lead to serious long-term complications such as cirrhosis and liver cancer.Hepatitis B poses the greater burden: About 240 million people worldwide live with it, and 1.1 million die from hepatitis B each year—more than twice as many as from malaria. About 240,000 people die from hepatitis C each year. And although the vast majority of hepatitis C cases have been curable since 2015, only about 20 percent of those affected have been treated since then, as the WHO pointed out on Tuesday on the occasion of World Hepatitis Day.According to the Federal Office of Public Health (FOPH), approximately 0.5 to 0.7 percent of the population in Switzerland is chronically infected with the hepatitis B virus. The number of reported cases of acute hepatitis B in Switzerland is showing a downward trend, the FOPH states on its website. The number of people infected with hepatitis C is also declining. Depending on the estimate, 0.1 to 0.4 percent of the population in Switzerland is chronically infected with the hepatitis C virus.Vaccination RecommendedThe WHO recommends routine hepatitis B testing for pregnant women—and vaccination for babies born to infected women immediately after birth. The FOPH also recommends vaccination at an early age. Vaccination is likewise recommended for at-risk groups of all ages. The federal government cites healthcare workers and people who use drugs as examples. There is no vaccine against hepatitis C.A study this spring gave hepatitis experts cause for hope: For the first time, a Chinese team succeeded in achieving a functional cure in some hepatitis B patients using combination therapy. A functional cure means that the viral load is below the detection limit—even though the virus could multiply again later, for example, if the immune system is compromised.Found an error?Report nowMore About this TopicArgentinaMilei Wants to Deport Foreigners for "Hate Speech"MID Games of ChanceFour lucky winners will receive 2,222 francs per month for five yearsUnbelievable Fraud in ItalyMan Builds an Amphitheater—and Rips Off Tourists for YearsFranceA warehouse containing old military ammunition explodes during a forest fireMost Read1French Farmhouse: George and Amal Clooney Evacuated Due to Wildfire2Hiker falls 80 meters into stream bed - Rega rescue takes hours3FIFA expert Tognoni: “Gianni Infantino is pursuing a two-pronged strategy”4Heat Wave Hits with Full Force – Everything You Need to Know Right Now5Ikea goods appear illegally in a shopping center in PyongyangWHO Calls for Greater Efforts in the Fight Against Hepatitis B and CWe Care About Your PrivacyWe and our 229 partners store and/or access information on a device, such as unique IDs in cookies to process personal data. WHO calls for greater efforts in fighting hepatitis B and C, including more research, testing, treatment, and political commitment. Hepatitis B poses a greater burden with 240 million cases and 1.1 million annual deaths.
Scale, impact and risk

Response, status and uncertainty
WHO recommends routine testing for pregnant women and vaccination for babies born to infected mothers. FOPH recommends early vaccination and for at-risk groups. No hepatitis C vaccine exists. A recent study showed functional cure potential for hepatitis B with combination therapy.

TAF 与 TDF 治疗慢性 HBV:疗效相当且安全性更高

TAF vs TDF for Chronic HBV: Comparable Efficacy With Improved Safety Profile
新闻简报一项比较 TAF 和 TDF 对慢性 HBV 感染的荟萃分析发现,它们在病毒抑制方面的功效相当,并且 TAF 证明了安全性结果的改善。与 TDF 相比,TAF 的丙氨酸转氨酶正常化率更高,骨矿物质密度和肾功能指标下降幅度更小。然而,TAF 与较差的 LDL 胆固醇结果相关。该分析基于对 1960 名患者进行的 4 项试验,强调了 TAF 在优化临床结果的同时最大限度地减少器官毒性的潜力,尽管其局限性包括试验赞助和较短的随访时间。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 7 月 28 日;研究终点在 48 周时评估。
地点与对象
所提供的证据中未指定;慢性乙型肝炎病毒感染患者。
事件
一项系统评价和荟萃分析,比较了富马酸替诺福韦艾拉酚胺 (TAF) 和富马酸替诺福韦二吡呋酯 (TDF) 治疗慢性 HBV 感染的情况,结果显示 TAF 的疗效相当,且安全性有所改善。
规模、影响与风险
Analysis included 4 randomized controlled trials with 1960 patients; viral suppression rates did not differ significantly between TAF and TDF groups (RR, 1.00; 95% CI, 0.96-1.05; P =.82).
应对、状态与不确定性
研究局限性包括试验数量少、2 项试验的偏倚风险高、试验之间的异质性、仅 48 周的随访以及所有试验均由制造商赞助;需要进行持续的调查来优化 治疗 策略。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

TAF vs TDF for Chronic HBV: Comparable Efficacy With Improved Safety Profile

News Brief
A meta-analysis comparing TAF and TDF for chronic HBV infection found comparable efficacy in viral suppression, with TAF demonstrating improved safety outcomes. TAF showed higher rates of alanine transaminase normalization and smaller reductions in bone mineral density and kidney function markers compared to TDF. However, TAF was associated with worse LDL cholesterol outcomes. The analysis, based on 4 trials with 1960 patients, highlights TAF's potential to optimize clinical outcomes while minimizing organ toxicity, though limitations include trial sponsorship and short follow-up duration.
Original source text (English)
Menu SECTIONS News Features CME Conferences Clinical Tools Special Collections Multimedia Fact Sheets Diagnosis & Disease Info Topics Abdominal Infections Bone and Joint Infections Cardiovascular Infections Central Nervous System Infections Covid 19 News & Insights Eye Infections Gastrointestinal Infections General Infectious Disease General Medicine Hepatitis HIV/AIDS Infection Control and Prevention Nosocomial Infections Pleuropulmonary and Bronchial Infections Practice Management Reproductive Organ Infections and Sexually Transmitted Infections Sepsis Skin and Soft Tissue Infection Upper Respiratory Tract Infections Urinary Tract Infection Vector-Borne Illnesses Haymarket Medical Network Cancer Therapy AdvisorClinical AdvisorClinical Pain AdvisorDermatology AdvisorEndocrinology AdvisorGastroenterology AdvisorHematology AdvisorInfectious Disease AdvisorMedical BagMPRNeurology AdvisorOncology Nurse AdvisorOphthalmology AdvisorOptometry AdvisorPsychiatry AdvisorPulmonology AdvisorRare Disease AdvisorRenal and Urology NewsRheumatology AdvisorSleep Wake AdvisorThe Cardiology AdvisorVaccine Advisor Search for: COVID-19 News CME Drugs Calculators Charts Resources Multimedia Meetings Part of the News and Features CME Conferences Clinical Tools Special Collections Multimedia Share this article Share on Facebook Share on Twitter Share on LinkedIn Share by Email Print Hepatitis TAF vs TDF for Chronic HBV: Comparable Efficacy With Improved Safety Profile Todd Neale, MA | Publish Date July 28, 2026 Tenofovir alafenamide fumarate achieves viral suppression rates comparable to tenofovir disoproxil fumarate in patients with chronic hepatitis B virus infection, with an improved safety profile, highlighting its potential to optimize clinical outcomes while minimizing organ toxicity. Tenofovir alafenamide fumarate (TAF) appears to be a safer option for the treatment of chronic hepatitis B virus (HBV) than tenofovir disoproxil fumarate (TDF), with similar efficacy, according to study findings published in BMC Infectious Diseases. Researchers conducted a systematic review and meta-analysis to synthesize head-to-head data comparing TAF and TDF for chronic HBV infection. The analysis included 4 randomized controlled trials with a total of 1960 patients. Mean patient age ranged from 38 to 56 years within the trials, which all assessed endpoints at 48 weeks. Two trials included only hepatitis B e-antigen (HBeAg)-negative patients, with the other 2 including both HBeAg-negative and -positive patients. The primary outcome was the suppression of HBV DNA to less than 15 to 29 IU/mL, which did not differ significantly between the TAF and TDF groups in a random-effects model (risk ratio [RR], 1.00; 95% CI, 0.96-1.05; P =.82). These insights underscore the need for ongoing investigation to optimize treatment strategies for CHB [chronic hepatitis B] patients. Patients who received TAF, however, were more likely to have a normalization of alanine transaminase levels (RR, 1.38; 95% CI, 1.16-1.64; P =.0002) and had smaller reductions in bone mineral density measured at the hip (mean difference [MD], 1.44; 95% CI, 0.98-1.91; P <.00001) and spine (MD, 1.93; 95% CI, 1.42-2.44; P <.00001) compared with those who received TDF. Receipt of TAF was also associated with a lesser increase in serum creatinine concentration (MD, -0.02; 95% CI, -0.03 to -0.01; P <.00001) and smaller drop in estimated glomerular filtration rate (MD, 3.55; 95% CI, 2.97-4.14; P <.00001). Patients who received TAF vs TDF experienced worse outcomes in regard to low-density lipoprotein (LDL) cholesterol levels, being approximately 5-fold more likely to have levels greater than 190 to 300 mg/dL (RR, 4.95; 95% CI, 1.21-20.29; P =.03). The researchers cautioned that “the estimate is uncertain due to a few events and wide confidence intervals.” Study limitations include the inability to explore additional outcomes related to bone health and kidney function or additional lipid parameters beyond LDL cholesterol, the relatively small number of trials, the high risk of bias in 2 of the trials, heterogeneity in clinical characteristics across trials, the inability to examine potential differences in longer-term outcomes due to follow-up lasting only 48 weeks, and the fact that all 4 trials were sponsored by the manufacturer of both medications. According to the researchers, “These insights underscore the need for ongoing investigation to optimize treatment strategies for CHB [chronic hepatitis B] patients.” References: Dar MS, Ovais H, Khan AW, et al. Efficacy and safety of tenofovir alafenamide versus tenofovir disoproxil in chronic hepatitis b: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials. BMC Infect Dis. 慢性乙型肝炎中的短链脂肪、胆汁酸信号

Short-Chain Fatty, Bile Acids Signal in Chronic Hep B
新闻简报《临床与转化肝病学杂志》最近发表的一篇评论探讨了 慢性乙型肝炎 (CHB) 中的肠肝轴,强调了微生物代谢物——短链脂肪酸 (SCFA) 和胆汁酸 (BA)——在调节 HBV 复制和免疫中的作用。丁酸盐等 SCFA 通过 HDAC 抑制和表观遗传重塑来抑制 HBV,而胆汁酸则通过 FXR 和 TGR5 发出信号,影响病毒转录和进入​​。该综述指出,目前的治疗很少能达到 功能性治愈,只有 1.4-20% 的患者实现 HBsAg 消失。新兴策略包括 SCFA 补充剂、FXR 激动剂和 PegIFN-α 联合疗法,但临床数据仍然有限。作者强调需要对患者进行分层和多目标方法来改善结果,同时承认剂量差距和潜在副作用等风险。

发表日期:2026-07-30。事件日期:提供的证据中未报告。

Affected population: ~254 million people with chronic hepatitis B (CHB).

《临床与转化肝病学杂志》上发表的关于短链脂肪酸 (SCFA) 和胆汁酸 (BA) 在 慢性乙型肝炎 感染中的作用的科学评论。该综述讨论了新出现的肠-肝轴证据,并提出了生物标志物引导的分层和多目标策略,以提高 功能性治愈 率。

CHB causes ~1.1 million annual deaths. Current therapies achieve functional cure (HBsAg loss) in only 1.4–20% of patients. The review highlights the potential of SCFAs and BAs as therapeutic targets but notes evidence is predominantly preclinical with limited clinical data.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Short-Chain Fatty, Bile Acids Signal in Chronic Hep B

News Brief
A recent review in the Journal of Clinical and Translational Hepatology explores the gut-liver axis in chronic hepatitis B (CHB), highlighting the role of microbial metabolites—short-chain fatty acids (SCFAs) and bile acids (BAs)—in modulating HBV replication and immunity. SCFAs like butyrate suppress HBV via HDAC inhibition and epigenetic remodelling, while bile acids signal through FXR and TGR5, influencing viral transcription and entry. The review notes that current therapies rarely achieve functional cure, with only 1.4–20% of patients achieving HBsAg loss. Emerging strategies include SCFA supplementation, FXR agonists, and combination therapies with PegIFN-α, though clinical data remains limited. The authors emphasize the need for patient stratification and multi-target approaches to improve outcomes, while acknowledging risks such as dosing gaps and potential side effects.
Original source text (English)
Mirage News Mirage News Mirage News Science 31 Jul 2026 12:30 am AEST Date Time Xia & He Publishing Inc. Chronic hepatitis B (CHB) remains a major global challenge. Current therapies suppress HBV but rarely achieve functional cure (sustained HBsAg loss). Emerging gut‐liver axis evidence shows that microbial metabolites—short‐chain fatty acids (SCFAs) and bile acids (BAs)—modulate HBV replication and immunity. Butyrate suppresses HBV via HDAC inhibition and cccDNA epigenetic remodelling. SCFAs may enhance antiviral immunity but can also reinforce tolerance. BAs signal through FXR, TGR5, and the HBV entry receptor NTCP, forming a dual‐effect hub. Early studies suggest FXR agonists combined with PegIFN‐α may reduce HBsAg. This review proposes biomarker‐guided stratification and multi‐target strategies to improve functional cure rates. Introduction CHB affects ~254 million people, causing ~1.1 million annual deaths. Functional cure (HBsAg loss with undetectable HBV DNA) is rarely achieved with current NAs or PegIFN‐α (HBsAg clearance rates: 1.4–5.1% for NAs, 2.1–20% for PegIFN‐α). Most patients need lifelong therapy. The gut‐liver axis offers new targets via SCFAs and BAs, which regulate viral replication and hepatic immunity. SCFAs in HBV Infection SCFAs (acetate, propionate, butyrate) are produced by colonic fermentation. Butyrate directly suppresses HBV in cell lines via HDAC inhibition, remodelling cccDNA chromatin, and may enhance p53 activity. However, effective in vitro concentrations are millimolar, far exceeding physiological micromolar levels—a key translational gap. Immunologically, SCFAs act through GPR41/43 and GPR109A: they can promote antiviral responses (T cells, NK cells, macrophages) and reduce hepatic stellate cell inflammation. Conversely, they may promote Treg differentiation and IL‐10/TGF‐β, reinforcing immune tolerance—particularly concerning in the immune‐tolerant phase. The net effect depends on disease stage and immune context. Bile Acids in HBV Infection Primary BAs are synthesised from cholesterol and converted to secondary BAs by gut microbiota. They signal through FXR (nuclear) and TGR5 (membrane). FXR has dual effects: physiological activation enhances HBV transcription (proviral), but sustained high‐dose FXR agonists may downregulate FXR and suppress HBV via negative feedback. FXR activation also downregulates NTCP, the HBV entry receptor, potentially reducing de novo infection. TGR5 activation reduces inflammation via cAMP/NF‐κB but has no direct antiviral effect; dual FXR/TGR5 agonists like INT‐767 may block viral entry by binding preS1. NTCP is both the BA uptake transporter and essential HBV receptor; its inhibition (e.g., bulevirtide) blocks entry but risks BA homeostasis disruption. SCFA‐BA Synergies and Therapeutic Strategies BAs can suppress butyrate‐producing bacteria, further reducing SCFAs in advanced fibrosis; SCFAs regulate BA synthesis via the FXR‐FGF19 axis and strengthen gut barrier. Thus, a self‐reinforcing cycle of dysbiosis, BA dysregulation, and SCFA depletion is a key therapeutic target. Therapeutic approaches include direct SCFA supplementation, prebiotics/probiotics/FMT, and FXR agonists (vonafexor, ASC42) in early trials. Combinations with PegIFN‐α may improve HBsAg decline. Future strategies include intestinal‐targeted FXR modulators and dual FXR/TGR5 agents. Metabolic‐immune combinations (e.g., SCFA prodrug + NA + immunotherapy) may target multiple mechanisms. Patient stratification by disease phase and gut microbiota/metabolic profiles is essential. Limitations and Risks Evidence is predominantly preclinical; clinical data are scarce and small‐scale. Dosing gaps exist (in vitro vs. physiological concentrations). FXR agonists may cause pruritus, dyslipidaemia, and cholestasis; SCFAs may cause GI effects or promote tolerance; probiotics/FMT carry infection risks. Long‐term viral adaptation and durability of HBsAg loss are unknown. Conclusion and Outlook Targeting SCFA and BA pathways offers a promising frontier for CHB. SCFAs suppress HBV epigenetically and modulate immunity; BAs influence viral transcription (FXR), entry (NTCP), and inflammation (TGR5). These axes are complementary, supporting multi‐target combination strategies. Future work should focus on multi‐omics profiling for stratification, humanised models, rigorous RCTs, and next‐generation delivery platforms. With careful development, metabolic‐immune combinations may improve functional cure rates and patient outcomes. Full text https://www.xiahepublishing.com/2310-8819/JCTH-2026-00118 The study was recently published in the Journal of Clinical and Translational Hepatology . The Journal of Clinical and Translational Hepatology (JCTH) is owned by the Second Affiliated Hospital of Chongqing Medical University and published by XIA & HE Publishing Inc. JCTH publishes high quality, peer reviewed studies in the translational and clinical human health sciences of liver diseases. JCTH has established high standards for publication of original research, which are characterized by a study's novelty, quality, and ethical conduct in the scientific process as well as in the communication of the research findings. Each issue includes articles by leading authorities on topics in hepatology that are germane to the most current challenges in the field. Special features include reports on the latest advances in drug development and technology that are relevant to liver diseases. Regular features of JCTH also include editorials, correspondences and invited commentaries on rapidly progressing areas in hepatology. All articles published by JCTH, both solicited and unsolicited, must pass our rigorous peer review process. /Public Release. Discover Saudi Leading Edge Powering Progress Experience Brazil Italy – Heritage & Horizons Poland Untold Costa Rica Beyond Business Saadiyat Cultural District My Tokyo Kerala Tourism Dubai Global Digital Economy Hub Turkmenistan - Our story Endless Discovery Expo 2025 Osaka, Kansai, Japan Services Weather Bulletin Messaging apps Widgets & Services Online Language Courses Newsletter Newsletters Events Events Podcasts Videos Africanews Loader Find Us ADVERTISEMENT Advertisement Health Health news Europe set to miss 2030 hepatitis elimination targets, health agency warns Europe to miss 2030 hepatitis elimination targets. - Copyright Cleared/Canva Copyright Cleared/Canva By Marta Iraola Iribarren Published on 28/07/2026 - 11:55 GMT+2 Share Comments Add Euronews on Google Share Facebook Twitter Flipboard Send Reddit Linkedin Messenger Telegram VK Bluesky Threads Whatsapp Despite years of progress, the ECDC says Europe is to miss its 2030 goal to eliminate viral hepatitis as a public health threat, as gaps in child vaccination and inadequate reduction measures are seen across Europe. Despite progress over the last few years in preventing hepatitis B and C, the European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control (ECDC) warns the region is off track to meet the 2030 elimination targets. ADVERTISEMENT ADVERTISEMENT ADVERTISEMENT “No one should die from a disease that is preventable, diagnosable and treatable, yet millions of Europeans are living with chronic hepatitis B or C infections – often without knowing it – which can lead to severe long-term consequences such as liver failure or liver cancer,” said Bruno Ciancio, Head of Unit for Directly Transmitted and Vaccine-Preventable Diseases at ECDC. Hepatitis B and C are transmitted through infected blood or bodily fluids and are among the leading causes of chronic liver disease. While acute hepatitis B infections have been falling steadily since 2006, driven by long-standing vaccination and prevention efforts, an estimated five million people in Europe are still living with chronic hepatitis B or C, and around 60,000 die every year from liver disease linked to these infections, according to the ECDC. Among people younger than 25 years, the proportion of acute hepatitis B cases fell from 11% in 2015 to 8% in 2024, reflecting the impact of childhood vaccination programmes and other prevention measures. Routine screening for hepatitis B in pregnancy is also well established across all 30 European Union and European Economic Area countries, helping to bring down transmission of the virus from mother to child to below 2%, the ECDC said. Ciancio noted that effective control solutions are already in place. Those include vaccination to prevent hepatitis B, harm reduction and infection control initiatives to reduce transmission, testing to identify infections early, and treatments that can cure hepatitis C and control hepatitis B. Related Millions in Europe unknowingly living with hepatitis, health authorities warn Gaps across Europe However, experts have identified gaps in routine childhood vaccination. Only a minority of countries have reached the World Health Organization (WHO) target of fully vaccinating 95% of children against hepatitis B, with others even reporting a decline in coverage in recent years. Harm reduction measures — such as opioid agonist treatment and the provision of sterile needles and syringes — are effective in limiting transmission of hepatitis C. Still, coverage remains inadequate, and only seven countries meet the WHO target for distributing sterile injection equipment to people who inject drugs. “Our collective challenge is to ensure that these life-saving interventions are accessible to everyone who needs them,” Ciancio said. Sexual transmission remains a major route of hepatitis B transmission. For hepatitis C, most cases are linked to injecting drug use and sex between men. Yet, the ECDC noted that specific prevention efforts targeting sexual transmission of hepatitis across different populations have not been systematically monitored across countries. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Hawaiʻi tackles liver cancer by taking on hepatitis

News Brief
Hawaiʻi is addressing high liver cancer rates by focusing on hepatitis prevention through education, testing, and vaccination. The state has one of the highest liver cancer rates in the US, with hepatitis B and C as primary causes. The Department of Health promotes vaccination for hepatitis A and B, encourages testing for at-risk groups, and supports the Hep Free Hawaiʻi campaign, which has identified previously undiagnosed cases. While vaccination and post-exposure treatment are available for hepatitis B, no vaccine exists for hepatitis C. Public health efforts aim to raise awareness and connect residents with care.
Original source text (English)
HONOLULU (KHON2) — A healthy liver works quietly every day, but diseases like viral hepatitis can do the same. Many people in Hawaiʻi do not know they have an infection until serious liver damage has already begun. That's why education, testing and vaccination have remained a key part of Hawaiʻi's effort to prevent hepatitis and reduce the risk of liver disease. World Hepatitis Day is observed every year on July 28. The day has been a means for raising awareness about viral hepatitis and encouraging people to learn about prevention, testing and treatment. In Hawaiʻi, the Department of Health has created a campaign and provided information to help residents understand how hepatitis spreads, who may be at risk and what steps can help prevent infection. Hawaiʻi is one of the states with the highest rates of liver cancer, and viral hepatitis B and hepatitis C are the leading causes. Viral hepatitis is a group of viruses that attack the liver. The most common types in Hawaiʻi are hepatitis A, hepatitis B and hepatitis C. Hepatitis A usually causes a short-term illness. It spreads through close personal contact or by consuming contaminated food or water. A vaccination offers protection, and good handwashing helps prevent the spread of the virus. Hepatitis B can begin as a short-term infection and may become a long-term illness. Chronic hepatitis B can lead to liver damage, cirrhosis, liver cancer and death. The virus spreads through infected blood, semen or other body fluids; and it can be passed from an infected mother to her baby during birth, through sex with an infected partner, by sharing needles or through contact with infected blood. The Department of Health noted that hepatitis B does not spread through food or water, sharing eating utensils, breastfeeding, hugging, kissing, holding hands, coughing or sneezing. They also said there is no vaccine for hepatitis C. The Department of Health identified vaccination as the best way to prevent hepatitis A and hepatitis B. The hepatitis A vaccine is given as two doses six months apart. The hepatitis B vaccine is given as a series of three shots over six months. Completing the full series provides long term protection. They also encouraged hepatitis B testing for people in several groups that include Household and sexual contacts of people with hepatitis B. Anyone who may have been exposed to hepatitis B should contact a health care provider immediately. The DOH said treatment soon after exposure may help prevent infection. The DOH also has been supporting education through Hep Free Hawaiʻi . The partnership has brought together doctors, community organizations and advocates to increase awareness, encourage testing and vaccination and connect people with medical care and support. Since the campaign began in 2011, Hep Free Hawaiʻi has held events across the state to promote hepatitis awareness and testing. On World Hepatitis Day in 2011, the DOH said a group tested 140 people and identified 10 who were positive for hepatitis and did not know they were infected. You can click here to access the DOH's page on viral hepatitis, here for their page on hepatitis B and here for hepatitis A. The DOH encouraged residents to learn about hepatitis, stay up to date on recommended vaccinations and talk with a health care provider about testing when appropriate. 肝炎:许多尼日利亚人将早期症状误认为是疟疾——专家

Hepatitis: Many Nigerians Mistake Early Symptoms For Malaria – Expert
新闻简报尼日利亚胃肠病学家警告说,许多当地人将早期肝炎症状误认为是疟疾,导致 诊断 延迟和严重的肝脏并发症。伊比蒂法官强调,不良的求医行为、意识不足和农村筛查机会有限是主要挑战。虽然乙型肝炎 疫苗 自 2004 年起就已推出,但在此之前出生的成年人仍然不受保护。专家呼吁扩大公众教育、基于社区的筛查和 疫苗接种 活动,以改善早期发现。丙型肝炎虽然无法通过 疫苗 治愈,但如果及早发现,是可以治疗的。农村地区服务的缺乏和 疫苗 的犹豫加剧了负担,许多人发现晚期肝损伤时为时已晚。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Hepatitis: Many Nigerians Mistake Early Symptoms For Malaria – Expert

News Brief
A Nigerian gastroenterologist warns that many locals mistake early hepatitis symptoms for malaria, leading to delayed diagnosis and severe liver complications. Dr. Justice Igbiti highlights poor health-seeking behavior, inadequate awareness, and limited rural screening access as key challenges. While the Hepatitis B vaccine has been available since 2004, adults born before then remain unprotected. The expert calls for expanded public education, community-based screening, and vaccination campaigns to improve early detection. Hepatitis C, though incurable by vaccine, can be treated if caught early. The lack of rural outreach and vaccine hesitancy exacerbates the burden, with many only discovering advanced liver damage too late.
Original source text (English)
Hausa Edition Podcast Conferences LeVogue Magazine Business News Print Advert Rates Online Advert Rates Contact Us Thursday, July 30, 2026 No Result View All Result Home News Politics Business Sport Football Health Entertainment Education Opinion Editorial Columns Others LeVogue Magazine Conferences National Economy Contact Us Hausa Edition Home News Politics Business Sport Football Health Entertainment Education Opinion Editorial Columns Others LeVogue Magazine Conferences National Economy Contact Us No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Hepatitis: Many Nigerians Mistake Early Symptoms For Malaria – Expert by Patience Ivie Ihejirika 1 hour ago in Health Share on WhatsAppShare on FacebookShare on XTelegram Many Nigerians infected with hepatitis unknowingly mistake its early symptoms for malaria, leading to delayed diagnosis and increasing the risk of severe liver complications, a consultant gastroenterologist, Dr Justice Igbiti, has said. Igbiti said poor health-seeking behaviour remains one of the biggest challenges in the fight against hepatitis, as many people fail to seek medical attention when symptoms first appear. According to him, hepatitis has two stages; acute and chronic, with the acute stage occurring within the first six months of infection. “The acute phase commonly presents with fever, body weakness and joint pains, which many people simply assume is malaria. As a result, they do not go to the hospital for proper testing,” he said. He explained that although many people recover from the acute phase, some progress to chronic hepatitis without realising it because the symptoms subside. RELATED NEWS Expert Warns Against Neglecting One’s Health Over 1,000 Persons Benefit From Free Medical Outreach Nigeria, Development Partners Push Sustainable Growth ln Pharmaceutical Industry “The danger is that by the time many people discover they have hepatitis, the disease may have already caused serious liver damage, including cirrhosis or liver cancer. Our goal is not to diagnose hepatitis when someone already has liver cancer or cirrhosis. We want to detect it early enough to prevent those complications,” he said. Igbiti identified inadequate awareness, limited access to screening in rural communities and vaccine hesitancy as other major factors driving the burden of hepatitis in Nigeria. He noted that many awareness campaigns and screening exercises are concentrated in urban areas, leaving people in hard-to-reach communities without access to testing and preventive services. “Our outreaches need to go beyond the cities. People in remote communities are not being reached, and even when they are, some are reluctant to take blood tests or receive vaccines because of misconceptions,” he said. The gastroenterologist urged governments and development partners to intensify public education, expand community-based screening and strengthen vaccination campaigns to improve hepatitis prevention and early detection. He explained that the federal government introduced the Hepatitis B vaccine into the National Programme on Immunisation in 2004, ensuring routine vaccination for newborns. However, he noted that many adults born before then remain unprotected and should be screened and vaccinated. “Most people born before 2004 did not benefit from routine vaccination at birth. All adults should know their hepatitis status, and those who are eligible should be vaccinated,” he said. Igbiti added that completing the recommended three-dose Hepatitis B vaccination regimen provides protection for more than 30 years, while immunity can be monitored through antibody titre testing, particularly among healthcare workers and other high-risk groups. He further noted that although there is no vaccine for Hepatitis C, the disease is curable if detected early, stressing that regular follow-up for infected persons is essential to prevent liver damage and improve treatment outcomes. 早期筛查如何使香港妇女免于家人罹患肝癌的命运 - 《南华早报》

How early screening saved Hong Kong woman from her family’s liver cancer fate - South China Morning Post
新闻简报A Hong Kong woman in her fifties credits early screening for saving her from her family's liver cancer fate. Knowing she carried the hepatitis B virus since her twenties, she underwent regular health check-ups, which were only disrupted during the Covid-19 pandemic. A 2024 check-up revealed a surging viral load and a liver tumor despite no pain, but early detection allowed successful tumor removal. Now on daily medication with stable readings, she has changed her lifestyle. Health authorities, noting 40% of Hong Kong's 410,000 hepatitis B patients are unaware of their condition, renewed screening calls on World Hepatitis Day. The patient acknowledges a long fight ahead but is grateful her mother's fate didn't become hers.
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-07-28。该患者的定期检查在Covid-19大流行期间受到干扰,她于2024年返回接受检查。
地点与对象
受影响人群是一名五十多岁的女性,携带乙型肝炎病毒。卫生当局称,该市 5.6% 的人口受到乙型肝炎的影响。
事件
一名有肝癌家族史的香港女子在二十多岁时得知自己携带乙型肝炎病毒后,接受了早期筛查和定期健康检查。尽管在 Covid-19 大流行期间检查受到干扰,但 2024 年的一次检查显示病毒载量激增,并存在肝脏肿瘤,并已成功切除。
规模、影响与风险
The woman's mother and grandmother died of liver cancer. Health authorities report that 40% of 410,000 hepatitis B patients in Hong Kong are unaware of their condition. The tumor was caught early, allowing successful removal and recovery.
应对、状态与不确定性
该患者现在每天服用药物,血液读数稳定,并彻底改变了她的生活方式。卫生当局在世界肝炎日再次呼吁尽早进行乙型肝炎筛查。患者承认前面还有一场漫长的战斗,需要增强力量。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

How early screening saved Hong Kong woman from her family’s liver cancer fate - South China Morning Post

News Brief
A Hong Kong woman in her fifties credits early screening for saving her from her family's liver cancer fate. Knowing she carried the hepatitis B virus since her twenties, she underwent regular health check-ups, which were only disrupted during the Covid-19 pandemic. A 2024 check-up revealed a surging viral load and a liver tumor despite no pain, but early detection allowed successful tumor removal. Now on daily medication with stable readings, she has changed her lifestyle. Health authorities, noting 40% of Hong Kong's 410,000 hepatitis B patients are unaware of their condition, renewed screening calls on World Hepatitis Day. The patient acknowledges a long fight ahead but is grateful her mother's fate didn't become hers.
Original source text (English)
How early screening saved Hong Kong woman from her family’s liver cancer fate Health authorities renew call for early hepatitis B screening, citing 40 per cent of 410,000 patients in city being unaware of condition Hongkonger Ms Lam has long known she is carrying a ticking time bomb after her mother and grandmother died of liver cancer. Her mother was completely asymptomatic until a late-stage diagnosis left no option for surgery. “I knew I carried the hepatitis B virus when I was in my twenties and had since then undergone regular health check-ups to manage the bomb,” the woman in her fifties recalled. Her regular checks were only disrupted during the Covid-19 pandemic. When she returned for a check-up in 2024, tests showed a surging viral load and a liver tumour despite her not feeling any pain. Caught early, the tumour was successfully removed, and she was back on her feet just days after the operation. Now taking daily medication with stable blood readings, she has overhauled her lifestyle. “Fortunately, what happened to my mother did not happen to me ... a long battle has begun, and I need to build my strength to fight it,” she said. On World Hepatitis Day on Tuesday, health authorities renewed calls for early screening and management of hepatitis B, which affects 5.6 per cent, or about one in 20, of the city’s population

Event
A Hong Kong woman with a family history of liver cancer underwent early screening and regular health check-ups after learning she carried the hepatitis B virus in her twenties. Despite disrupted check-ups during the Covid-19 pandemic, a 2024 check-up revealed a surging viral load and a liver tumor, which was successfully removed.
Scale, impact and risk
The woman's mother and grandmother died of liver cancer. Health authorities report that 40% of 410,000 hepatitis B patients in Hong Kong are unaware of their condition. The tumor was caught early, allowing successful removal and recovery.
Response, status and uncertainty
The patient is now taking daily medication with stable blood readings and has overhauled her lifestyle. Health authorities renewed calls for early hepatitis B screening on World Hepatitis Day. The patient acknowledges a long battle ahead and the need to build strength.
